People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Um... well. I guess I'm just having some trouble with... doubts. I just keep getting this feeling that I've missed something important. 

Like... a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof at work. Or even just a door not being latched. And... even when I know I already checked it, the thought just kind of forces its way back in. Just this nagging idea of... what if this is the mistake that costs you everything? 

So... yeah. I suppose that's what brings me in.

I just have to be sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um... well. I guess I'm just having some trouble with... doubts. I just keep getting this feeling that I've missed something important. \n\nLike... a number on the mortgage payment, or a clause in a client proof at work. Or even just a door not being latched. And... even when I know I already checked it, the thought just kind of forces its way back in. Just this nagging idea of... what if this is the mistake that costs you everything? \n\nSo... yeah. I suppose that's what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to get rid of these doubts?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah. I suppose I do. \n\nI just... I'll end up reopening files at work. Or rereading emails. With the bills, I'll check the online payment confirmation... a few times. And sometimes I just sort of... say the figures silently in my head. Just until they feel... settled, I guess. \n\nIt's just... it turns a really simple invoice, or a basic logo revision, into hours of work. Hours I can't even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform physical actions, like checking doors or locks, over and over?\n\nPatient: Um... sometimes the doors, I guess. But... like I was just saying, it's mostly the work things. \n\nGoing back into the design files... pulling up the emails again. Staring at the payment confirmations. I just... I mostly just repeat the figures in my head until they feel right. That's what ends up wasting all those hours. So... mostly just that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these doubts come up?\n\nPatient: Um... yes. I suppose so. It's... unsettling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to repeat specific words or numbers to yourself over and over?\n\nPatient: Yes... um, like I mentioned a moment ago. With the figures. I'll just keep saying the numbers silently in my head until things feel... settled. It's... that's what turns a simple invoice into all those unbillable hours. I just keep reopening the files or the emails to check, and repeating them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like repeating those numbers in your head helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um... I suppose so. Yes. It makes things feel... settled. \n\nLike I was saying, I just end up reopening the files, or checking the online payment confirmation again... saying the figures silently to myself. It helps for a little bit, but... then I realize I've spent hours on a simple logo revision or an invoice that I can't even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't repeat those numbers in your head or check those things over and over?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know about something bad happening, exactly. It's just... I can't leave it alone. If I don't reopen the files or reread the emails... or check the payment confirmations again... it just feels wrong. So I keep doing it, and saying the numbers to myself, until it finally feels settled. Even though it means I waste hours I can't bill for.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't repeat those numbers or check things over and over, something specific would go wrong?\n\nPatient: I... I suppose it's just these awful doubts. That I've missed something important. \n\nLike... a clause in a client proof, or a number on the mortgage payment. Or even just a door not being latched. It's... even when I know I checked it, the thought just barges back in. Just this feeling of... what if this is the mistake that costs you everything? \n\nSo... I just have to be sure.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and repetitive behaviors affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't really know. \n\nI mean, I mostly just keep to myself when I'm working. So... I guess I haven't really noticed it causing any big problems with anyone. It's just... my own thing to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and repetitive behaviors affecting your work or daily life in any significant way?\n\nPatient: I... I suppose so. Mostly with my work. \n\nIt's just... things that used to take me an afternoon... they drag on for days now. So I've had to push back some deadlines. And... like I mentioned, I'm missing billable hours. \n\nIt's getting to be a problem. One of my longtime clients actually asked recently if I can still handle simple revisions on time. So... yes. It's making things difficult.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulties in your personal relationships due to these doubts and repetitive behaviors?\n\nPatient: I... didn't we just talk about that? \n\nLike I said a minute ago... I mostly just keep to myself. My life is pretty quiet outside of work. So... no. I don't really have anything to add about that.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these doubts and repetitive behaviors to you?\n\nPatient: I don't really... I don't know. \n\nI guess I don't really think about it in those terms. It's... frustrating, I suppose. But I just try to get through it. I don't really focus on how it makes me feel.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these doubts and the time you spend repeating things in your head are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I... I think I just answered that. \n\nLike I said... I don't really focus on the distress part of it. It's just... something I have to deal with. I don't really know what else to say about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And sometimes I just sort of... say the figures silently in my head. But... like I was just saying, it's mostly the work things. \n\nGoing back into the design files... pulling up the emails again. Staring at the payment confirmations. I just... I mostly just repeat the figures in my head until they feel right. That's what ends up wasting all those hours. So... mostly just that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these doubts come up?\n\nPatient: Um... yes. I suppose so. It's... unsettling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to repeat specific words or numbers to yourself over and over?\n\nPatient: Yes... um, like I mentioned a moment ago. With the figures. I'll just keep saying the numbers silently in my head until things feel... settled. It's... that's what turns a simple invoice into all those unbillable hours. I just keep reopening the files or the emails to check, and repeating them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like repeating those numbers in your head helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um... I suppose so. Yes. It makes things feel... settled. \n\nLike I was saying, I just end up reopening the files, or checking the online payment confirmation again... saying the figures silently to myself. It helps for a little bit, but... then I realize I've spent hours on a simple logo revision or an invoice that I can't even bill for.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't repeat those numbers in your head or check those things over and over?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know about something bad happening, exactly. It's just... I can't leave it alone. I just have to be sure.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and repetitive behaviors affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't really know. \n\nI mean, I mostly just keep to myself when I'm working. So... I guess I haven't really noticed it causing any big problems with anyone. It's just... my own thing to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and repetitive behaviors affecting your work or daily life in any significant way?\n\nPatient: I... I suppose so. I mostly just keep to myself. My life is pretty quiet outside of work. So... no. I think I just answered that. \n\nLike I said... I don't really focus on the distress part of it. It's just... something I have to deal with. I don't really know what else to say about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific physical rituals or actions in response to your doubts, beyond just checking doors or locks?\n\nPatient: I don't know if you'd call them rituals. I suppose it's just these awful doubts. That I've missed something important. \n\nLike... a clause in a client proof, or a number on the mortgage payment. Or even just a door not being latched. It's... even when I know I checked it, the thought just barges back in. Just this feeling of... what if this is the mistake that costs you everything? \n\nSo... I just have to be sure.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and repetitive behaviors affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't really know. \n\nI mean, I mostly just keep to myself when I'm working. So... I guess I haven't really noticed it causing any big problems with anyone. It's just... my own thing to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Are these doubts and repetitive behaviors affecting your work or daily life in any significant way?\n\nPatient: I... I suppose so. Mostly with my work. \n\nIt's just... things that used to take me an afternoon... they drag on for days now. So I've had to push back some deadlines. And... like I mentioned, I'm missing billable hours. \n\nIt's getting to be a problem. One of my longtime clients actually asked recently if I can still handle simple revisions on time. So... yes. It's making things difficult.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulties in your personal relationships due to these doubts and repetitive behaviors?\n\nPatient: I... didn't we just talk about that? \n\nLike I said a minute ago... I mostly just keep to myself. My life is pretty quiet outside of work. So... no. I don't really have anything to add about that.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these doubts and repetitive behaviors to you?\n\nPatient: I don't really... I don't know. \n\nI guess I don't really think about it in those terms. It's... frustrating, I suppose. But I just try to get through it. I don't really focus on how it makes me feel.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these doubts and the time you spend repeating things in your head are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I... I think I just answered that. \n\nLike I said... I don't really focus on the distress part of it. It's just... something I have to deal with. I don't really know what else to say about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific physical rituals or actions in response to your doubts, beyond just checking doors or locks?\n\nPatient: I don't know if you'd call them rituals. It's mostly just... work stuff. \n\nI end up reopening my design files a lot. Or rereading emails. Checking the online payment confirmations over and over. Sometimes I... I just say the figures silently in my head until things feel... settled, I guess. \n\nIt just means a simple invoice or a logo revision can turn into hours I can't bill for. But... yeah. That's mostly what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to repeat any specific words or phrases, other than numbers, in your head to alleviate your doubts?\n\nPatient: No... not words. Like I just said, it's really just the figures. I just say the numbers in my head until it feels settled. And... going back into the files, or rereading the emails. That's really all it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you didn't repeat these numbers or check things over and over, a specific bad thing would happen?\n\nPatient: No... not really. I don't picture some specific disaster happening, if that's what you mean. \n\nIt just... it wouldn't feel right if I didn't do it. Like I said, I just need it to feel settled before I can move on. That's all it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.
